                .addSize([320, 100], [[300, 50], [320, 50]])
                .addSize([740, 100], [728, 90])
                .addSize([0, 0], 'fluid')
                .build(),
            mobileleaderboard: googletag.sizeMapping()
                .addSize([300, 100], [300, 50])
                .addSize([320, 100], [320, 50])
                .build(),
            mobilefooter: googletag.sizeMapping()
                .addSize([0, 0], [300, 50])
                .addSize([0, 0], [320, 50])
                .build(),
            rail: googletag.sizeMapping()
                .addSize([0, 0], [300, 250])
                .build(),
            interstitial: googletag.sizeMapping()
                .addSize([0, 0], [640, 480])
                .build()
        };

        gptPage = window.digitalData && digitalData.page && digitalData.page.page ? digitalData.page.page : "blank";
        gptSubPage = window.digitalData && digitalData.page && digitalData.page.subPage ? digitalData.page.subPage : "blank";

        function gptClean(gptText) {
            if (typeof (gptText) == "string") {
                gptText = gptText.replace(/\&/g, '%26');
                return gptText.replace(/\^|\*|#|\$|\"|\'|\=|\,|\!|\+|\~|\;|\(|\)|\<|\>|\[|\]|\?|\%|\:|\@|\;|\&|\||\{|\}|\\|/g, '');
            }
            return "blank";
        }

        const identities = window.adobeDataLayer?.[0]?.user?.identities || [];
        const segments = identities
            .filter(identity => identity.type === 'AudienceSegmentsUser')
            .map(identity => identity.name)
            .filter(name => name);

        googletag.pubads()
            .setTargeting('property', 'value-of-property')    // Change and duplicate this line for as many additional keywords to send
            .setTargeting('doi', gptClean('10.1002/smll.202401282')) // Publication DOI
            .setTargeting('journalDoi', gptClean('10.1002/(ISSN)1613-6829')) // journal DOI
            .setTargeting('journalCode', gptClean('16136829')) // journal code
            .setTargeting('pbPage', gptClean(gptPage))
            .setTargeting('pbSubPage', gptClean(gptSubPage))
            .setTargeting('AudienceSegmentsUser', segments)
            .setTargeting('urlpath', gptClean(document.location.pathname))
			.setTargeting('env', window.adobeDataLayer?.[0]?.site?.environment || 'n/a');


        googletag.defineSlot($GooglePublisherCategory, 'fluid', 'advert-leaderboard')
            .defineSizeMapping(sizes.leaderboard)
            .addService(googletag.pubads())
            .setCollapseEmptyDiv(true);

        if(window.innerWidth >= 992) { // Remove MPU ad units from mobile view (LIT-621710)
            googletag.defineSlot($GooglePublisherCategory, 'fluid', 'advert-rail-1')
                .defineSizeMapping(sizes.rail)
                .setTargeting('Position', 'advert-rail-1')
                .addService(googletag.pubads())
                .setCollapseEmptyDiv(true);

            googletag.defineSlot($GooglePublisherCategory, 'fluid', 'advert-rail-2')
                .defineSizeMapping(sizes.rail)
                .setTargeting('Position', 'advert-rail-2')
                .addService(googletag.pubads())
                .setCollapseEmptyDiv(true);
        }

		onDvtagReady();	// DoubleVerify (PERICLES-19717)

        if(isProgrammaticAdsEnabled) {
            // PERICLES-17135
            googletag.pubads().disableInitialLoad();
        }
		
        googletag.enableServices();
    });

    if (isProgrammaticAdsEnabled) {
        googletag.cmd.push(function () {
            apstag.fetchBids({timeout: 2000}, function (bids) {
                apstag.setDisplayBids();
                onDvtagReady(function () {
					setTimeout(() => {
						googletag.pubads().refresh();
					}, 300);
                })
            });
        });
    }

<!-- loading Javascript for using the persistence Service -->
    <script src="/wro/f5e7ffe6d6d1192a4defdde88dc425c93326c6ed~thiss-ds.js" nonce="9f00f37b24b5fe15-SJC"></script>

    <script type="text/javascript" nonce="9f00f37b24b5fe15-SJC">

        function getParameter(parameterName) {
            let result = null,
                tmp = [];
            location.search
                .substr(1)
                .split("&")
                .forEach(function (item) {
                    tmp = item.split("=");
                    if (tmp[0] === parameterName) result = tmp[1];
                });
            return result;
        }

        var SeamlessAccessService = {
            spUrl: "/action/ssostart",
            returnURL: window.location.href,
            my_context: 'seamlessaccess.org',
            queryParamsDelimiter: encodeURIComponent('?'),
            _persistenceService: null,
            _getPersistenceService: function () {
                if (!this._persistenceService) {
                    this._persistenceService = new thiss.PersistenceService('https://service.seamlessaccess.org/ps/');
                }
                return this._persistenceService;
            },

            getWAYFLessUrl: function (entityId) {
                let redirectURL = getParameter('redirectUri');
                if(redirectURL == null){
                    redirectURL = encodeURIComponent(this.returnURL);
                }
                if(redirectURL.indexOf('saml_referrer') < 0){
                    redirectURL += encodeURIComponent(((redirectURL.indexOf(this.queryParamsDelimiter) !== -1) ? '&' : '?') + 'saml_referrer');
                }
                return this.spUrl + "?idp=" + encodeURIComponent(entityId)
                    + "&redirectUri=" + redirectURL;
            },
            retrieveEntity: function () {
                var self = this;
                var ps = this._getPersistenceService();

                return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
                    ps.entities(self.my_context)
                        .then(function (res) {
                            // get the results
                            var results = res.data;
                            return resolve(results);
                        }, function (err) {
                            // failed
                            reject('retrieve entity error: ' + err);
                        });
                });
            },
            updateEntity: function (entitydata) {
                var self = this;
                var ps = this._getPersistenceService();

                var jsonObj = {};
                try {
                    //try to parse it, if it is a json string
                    jsonObj = JSON.parse(entitydata.val());
                } catch (e) {
                    jsonObj = entitydata;
                }
                return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
                    ps.update(self.my_context, jsonObj)
                        .then(function (res) {
                            // Process the results
                            var ssoInstitutions = res.data;
                            return resolve(ssoInstitutions);
                        }, function (err) {
                            // failed
                            console.log('failed to update the local storage duo to: ' + err);
                            reject(err);
                        });
                })
            },
            removeEntity: function (entityId) {
                var self = this;
                var persistService = this._getPersistenceService();

                return new Promise(function (resolve) {
                    persistService.remove(self.my_context, entityId)
                        .then(function (res) {
                            return resolve(res);
                        }, function (err) {
                            // failed
                            console.log('failed to remove from local storage duo to: ' + err);
                        });
                })
            },
            getAccessibleEntityStatus: function (doi, entityId, entityTitle) {
                return new Promise(function (resolve) {
                    $.ajax({
                        type: 'POST',
                        url: '/action/seamlessAccess',
                        accept: {
                            text: "application/json"
                        },
                        data: {doi: doi, entityid: entityId, entityTitle: entityTitle, eventGroupKey: '5eeb9de3-4f4a-4254-a752-82d8e9a08e02'}

                    }).done(function (status) {
                        return resolve(status);
                    });
                });
            },
            getEntitiesWithAllInfo: function (doi) {
                let self = this,
                    arrayOfPromises = [];
                return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
                    self.retrieveEntity().then(function (res) {
                        let allSsoInstitutions = res;
                        for (let i in allSsoInstitutions) {
                            let ssoInstitution = allSsoInstitutions[i];
                            arrayOfPromises.push(new Promise(function (resolve) {
                                self.getAccessibleEntityStatus(doi, ssoInstitution.entity.entityID, ssoInstitution.entity.title)
                                    .then(function (entityStatus) {
                                        resolve({
                                            entityID: ssoInstitution.entity.entityID,
                                            entityTitle: ssoInstitution.entity.title,
                                            entityStatusObj: entityStatus,
                                            entitySsoUrl: self.getWAYFLessUrl(ssoInstitution.entity.entityID)
                                        });
                                    }, function (err) {
                                        console.log('failed to get the entity status: ' + err);
                                    });
                            }));
                        }
                        Promise.all(arrayOfPromises)
                            .then(function (finalResponse) {
                                resolve(finalResponse);
                            });

                    }).catch(function (err) {
                        reject(err);
                    })
                });
            }
        };

        window.addEventListener('load', function () {
            document.querySelectorAll(".sso-institution").forEach(function (institution) {
                var entityId = institution.getAttribute('data-entityid');
                var entityName = institution.getAttribute('data-name');
                var entitydata = {
                    entityID: entityId,
                    title: entityName
                };
                institution.addEventListener("click", function (e) {
                    e.preventDefault();
                    var self = this;
                    SeamlessAccessService.updateEntity(entitydata).then(function (res) {
                        console.log(res);
                        window.location.href = self.href;
                    }).catch(function (err){
                        window.location.href = self.href;
                    });
                });
            });
        });

    </script>

        (function() {
            function riskifiedBeaconLoad() {
                var store_domain = 'https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com';
                var session_id = 'CD96C1FDA8A5F1675C023092203C8CD2';
                var url = ('https:' == document.location.protocol ? 'https://' : 'http://')
                    + "beacon.riskified.com?shop=" + store_domain + "&sid=" + session_id;
                var s = document.createElement('script');
                s.type = 'text/javascript';
                s.async = true;
                s.src = url;
                var x = document.getElementsByTagName('script')[0];
                x.parentNode.insertBefore(s, x);
            }
            if (window.attachEvent)
                window.attachEvent('onload', riskifiedBeaconLoad)
            else
                window.addEventListener('load', riskifiedBeaconLoad, false);
        })();

China</p><a class="moreInfoLink" href="/authored-by/Gao/Xueyun">Search for more papers by this author</a></div></span><span class="accordion-tabbed__tab-mobile  accordion__closed"><a href="/authored-by/Su/Dongdong" class="author-name accordion-tabbed__control" data-id="a9" data-db-target-for="a9" aria-controls="a9" aria-haspopup="true" id="a9_Ctrl" role="button"><span>Dongdong Su<i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-mail_outline"></i></span><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-section_arrow_d"></i></a><span class="comma-separator">,&nbsp;</span><div class="author-info accordion-tabbed__content" data-db-target-of="a9" aria-labelledby="a9_Ctrl" role="region" id="a9">
                     <p class="author-type mb-1">Corresponding Author</p>
                     <p class="author-name">Dongdong Su</p>
                     <ul class="rlist sm-account">
                        <li class="sm-account__item"><a class="sm-account__link" title="Link to email address" href="/cdn-cgi/l/email-protection#0c6f64617f7968684c6e66797822696879226f62"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-ic_mail_solid"></i><span><span class="__cf_email__" data-cfemail="fc9f94918f899898bc9e968988d2999889d29f92">[email&#160;protected]</span></span></a></li>

                     </ul>

   <div class="abstract-group ">
      <section class="article-section article-section__abstract" lang="en" data-lang="en" lang-name="English" id="section-1-en">
         <h2 id="d80835725" class="article-section__header section__title main abstractlang_en main">Abstract</h2>
         <div class="article-section__content en main">
            
            <p>Activatable near-infrared (NIR) fluorogenic probes offer a potent tool for real-time, in situ detection of hepatic biomarkers, significantly advancing the precision in diagnosing inflammatory liver disease (ILD). However, the limited distribution of small molecule fluorogenic probes in the liver and their rapid clearance impair the accuracy of fluorescence imaging and in ILD diagnosis. In this study, an effective utilization of ionizable lipid nanoparticles (iLNPs) is presented as liver-targeted carriers for efficient delivery of fluorogenic probes, aiming to overcome biodistribution barriers and achieve accurate detection of hepatic biomarkers. Based on this strategy, a liver-targeted NIR fluorogenic nanoprobe hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP is prepared using hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> as a small molecule reporter for visualizing the over-produced hydrogen peroxide (H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>) in situ of liver. Notably, iLNPs not only significantly enhance probe accumulation in the liver, but also enable sequence activation of fluorescent nanoprobes. This response is achieved through primary liposome-dissociation release and secondary hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> response with pathological H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, enabling high-precision detection of oxidative stress in hepatocytes. These distinctive features facilitate accurate early diagnosis of acetaminophen (APAP)-induced inflammatory liver injury as well as lipopolysaccharide (LPS)-induced hepatitis. Therefore, the organ-targeted nanoprobe design strategy showcasts great potential for early and accurate diagnosis of lesions in situ in different organs.</p>
            </div>
      </section>
   </div>
   <div class="pb-dropzone" data-pb-dropzone="below-abstract-group"><!-- Empty dropzone --></div>
   
   <section class="article-section article-section__full">
      <section class="article-section__content" id="smll202401282-sec-0010">
         
         <h2 class="article-section__title section__title section1" id="smll202401282-sec-0010-title">1 Introduction</h2>
         
         <p>Inflammatory liver disease (ILD), including hepatitis, fatty liver, and cirrhosis, is a group of diseases primarily characterized by liver inflammation caused by adverse factors such as infections, immune issues, medications, and toxins.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0001" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0001_R_d80835714e448" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 1 - J. Clin. Invest. Hepatology Metabolism BMJ">1</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Current diagnosis relies on blood tests and liver biopsies, which pose challenges in detecting pathological conditions at an early stage.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0002" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0002_R_d80835714e453" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 2 - Clin. Microbiol. Rev. Hepatology">2</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Oxidative stress is considered an initial event in inflammation marked by excessive concentrations of reactive oxygen species (ROS) that surpass the rate of cellular clearance, leading to tissue inflammation and damage.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0003" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0003_R_d80835714e458" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 3 - Chem. Soc. Rev. Trends. Mol. Med.">3</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Thus, ROS plays a pivotal role in assessing oxidative stress in hepatocytes and revealing the occurrence and progression of ILD. However, the high reactivity and short half-life of ROS make detecting ROS in vivo quite challenging.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0004" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0004_R_d80835714e464" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 4 - J. Am. Chem. Soc. Bioact. Mater.">4</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup></p>
         
         <p>The noninvasiveness, exceptional sensitivity, and selectivity of activatable NIR fluorogenic probes facilitate precise measurement of ROS levels and their distribution in vivo, showcasing their potential as ideal diagnostic systems for non-invasive in situ detection of oxidative stress-related diseases.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0005" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0005_R_d80835714e471" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 5 - Annu. Rev. Biochem. Chem. Sci.">5</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> However, the accuracy of in situ fluorescence imaging of hepatic biomarkers and ILD diagnosis is compromised under in situ imaging conditions due to low liver distribution and rapid metabolism of small molecule probes.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0006" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0006_R_d80835714e476" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 6 - Angew. Chem., Int. Ed. Chem. Rev. J. Am. Chem. Soc.">6</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> To address this issue, organ-targeted delivery has emerged as a commonly used strategy in biomedical field to improve the concentration and retention time of contrast agents specifically within the organ of interest.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0007" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0007_R_d80835714e481" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 7 - Adv. Mater. Proc. Natl. Acad. Sci. USA Nat. Rev. Mater.">7</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Moreover, the ultralow signals exhibited by these contrast agents in normal tissue are attributed to their rapid clearance properties from non-targeted regions.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0008" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0008_R_d80835714e487" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 8 - ACS Nano Proc. Natl. Acad. Sci. USA">8</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> In contrast to small molecule probes with unpredictable biodistribution, these unique features enable high-contrast imaging signals primarily within disease regions, resulting significantly enhanced accuracy and precision of in vivo imaging. Organ-targeted delivery mainly relies on nanoscale-dependent passive targeting and receptor-ligand mediated active targeting mechanisms that partially confer organ-specific targeting ability to the probes.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0009" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0009_R_d80835714e492" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 9 - Nat. Commun. Nat. Protoc. Small">9</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> However, the full potential of these strategies for achieving accurate detection of ROS in vivo is restricted by limitations such as non-specific signal associated with untimely activation and protein adsorption.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0010" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0010_R_d80835714e498" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 10 - Nat. Commun. Proc. Natl. Acad. Sci. USA Adv. Mater.">10</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup></p>
         
         <p>Recently reported ionizable lipid nanoparticles (iLNPs) have demonstrated their efficacy as a highly efficient platform for delivering molecules.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0011" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0011_R_d80835714e505" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 11 - Adv. Mater. Adv. Funct. Mater. Adv. Drug Delivery Rev.">11</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> iLNPs, characterized by a specific proportioned bilayer phospholipid structure, recruit specific proteins to their surface as targeting ligands, facilitating the effective internalization of cargo by specific categories of cells.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0012" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0012_R_d80835714e510" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 12 - Acc. Chem. Res. Angew. Chem., Int. Ed. Nat. Nanotechnol.">12</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Subsequently, the ionizable lipids within iLNPs undergo protonation in the acidic environment of endosomes or lysosomes when the pH below their p<i>K</i>a. The protonated lipids are more easy to disrupt the integrity of the natural bilayer membrane through the formation of lipid-ion pairs with negatively charged lipids present in the lysosomal membrane.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0013" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0013_R_d80835714e518" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 13 - J. Controlled Release Acc. Chem. Res. J. Mater. Chem. B">13</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> This process enhances the efficiency of cargo delivery into specific cells. Specific constituents of iLNPs can facilitate targeted delivery to specific organs, such as liver, lung, and spleen, thereby exhibiting significant potential for both fundamental research and clinical applications.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0014" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0014_R_d80835714e523" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 14 - Nat. Commun. Nat. Nanotechnol.">14</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Huang group has reported a series of iLNPs capable of achieving organ-targeted delivery and successfully demonstrated specific siRNA delivery to hepatocytes through rational design and high-throughput screening in vivo.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0015" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0015_R_d80835714e528" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 15 - Bioact. Mater. Bioact. Mater. Exploration Sci. Adv.">15</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> The majority of iLNPs reported to date have been utilized for the delivery of nucleic acid drugs in therapeutic applications. However, the utilization of molecular probe-loaded iLNPs targeted specific organs holds immense potential for achieving precise disease diagnosis. Inspired by these remarkable effects, iLNPs can serve as organ-targeted delivery vehicles that enable imaging agents to concentrate more effectively in specific regions of interest and facilitate reactions between probes with short half-life ROS within cells. Notably, the cytomembrane-mimicking iLNPs enables stable cargo encapsulation, thereby preventing premature activation by low-level ROS present in non-hepatic tissues prior to hepatic delivery of imaging agents. Consequently, liver-targeted iLNPs not only facilitate direct delivery of activatable NIR fluorogenic probes to hepatocytes, but also confer a sequence-activated feature on the imaging nanosystem, enhancing high-contrast imaging, early diagnosis, and monitoring oxidative stress in ILD.</p>
         
         <p>To accurately evaluate oxidative stress within inflammatory liver, a liver-targeted sequence-activated fluorogenic nanoprobe hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP was meticulously designed for the detection of ROS biomarkers H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> (<b>Scheme</b> <a href="#smll202401282-fig-0007">1</a>). In this study, we employed previously reported iLNPs composed of ionizable lipids, cholesterol, 1,2-dioctadecanoyl-sn-glycero-3-phophocholine (DSPC), and 1,2-dimyristoyl-rac-glycero-3-methoxypolyethylene glycol-2000 (DMG-PEG2000) in optimized proportions that spontaneously form a bilayer membrane structure.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0015" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0015_R_d80835714e550" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 15 - Bioact. Mater. Bioact. Mater. Exploration Sci. Adv.">15</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> This iLNPs demonstrate exceptional stability, loading efficiency, and liver specificity, thereby ensuring efficient delivery to the liver. Initially, a ROS-responsive NIR fluorogenic agent hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> was prepared and subsequently encapsulated in iLNPs to produce the ultimate liver-targeted sequence-activated fluorogenic nanoprobe hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP. Surprisingly, the encapsulation of cytomembrane-like iLNPs could effectively quench the fluorescence of imaging agents, as confirmed in vitro and in living cells, proving that the sequence activation of nanoprobe significantly reduces interference from non-specific fluorescence signals and enhances the precision in ILD imaging. Upon administration of nanoprobes, apolipoprotein E recruitment by iLNPs carriers predominates liver targeted delivery of caged fluorogenic agents into hepatocytes, thereby significantly enhancing their accumulation in the liver.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0016" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0016_R_d80835714e564" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 16 - Mol. Ther. Nucleic Acids">16</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Following release of cargo into the hepatocytes, pathological levels of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> detach the fluorescent quencher in hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, triggering a high-contrast indicative fluorescence signal (Scheme&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0007">1</a>). In ILD mice, hepatic ROS levels can be dynamically monitored using hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP, providing valuable insights into hepatic oxidative stress and inflammatory conditions. Therefore, the sequence-activated fluorescent nanoprobes formed by integrating activatable fluorogenic agents with liver-targeted iLNPs carriers represents a powerful strategy that effectively enhances in vivo imaging effects while offering precise information for unveiling biological details and facilitating accurate ILD diagnosis.</p>
         
         <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
            <figure class="figure" id="smll202401282-fig-0007"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/c2279278-e3e3-42c2-bbe5-17e81c395450/smll202401282-fig-0007-m.jpg"><picture>
                     <source srcset="/cms/asset/c2279278-e3e3-42c2-bbe5-17e81c395450/smll202401282-fig-0007-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/6ca54bd3-a7a8-4954-8478-d14cbd5bb37e/smll202401282-fig-0007-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/c2279278-e3e3-42c2-bbe5-17e81c395450/smll202401282-fig-0007-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                  <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">Scheme 1<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=smll202401282-fig-0007&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fsmll.202401282" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                  </div>
                  <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">Construction of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP and the proposed liver-targeted delivery and sensing mechanism of sequence-activated hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP to H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> in inflammatory liver diseases.</div>
               </figcaption>
            </figure>
         </section>
      </section>
      <section class="article-section__content" id="smll202401282-sec-0020">
         
         <h2 class="article-section__title section__title section1" id="smll202401282-sec-0020-title">2 Results and Discussion</h2>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="smll202401282-sec-0030">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="smll202401282-sec-0030-title">2.1 Synthesis and Spectral Characterization of Small Molecule Agent hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub></h3>
            
            <p>First, the ROS-responsive small molecular fluorogenic agent hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> was synthesized according to the detailed synthesis route shown in Scheme <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S1</a> (Supporting Information). hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> consists of a highly emissive hemi-cyanine (hCy) fluorophore coupled with an H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-specific phenylboronic acid moiety, enabling selective detection of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> along with release of a high-contrast fluorescence signal in the NIR region. The structure of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> was well confirmed by high-resolution electrospray ionization mass spectrometry (HRMS-ESI) (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S21</a>, Supporting Information) and nuclear magnetic resonance spectra (<sup>1</sup>H NMR and <sup>13</sup>C NMR, Figures <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S23</a> and <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S24</a>, Supporting Information). Prior to iLNPs encapsulation, the photophysical properties of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> were evaluated. hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> exhibited a broad absorbance centered around 598&nbsp;nm and displayed weak fluorescence in the NIR region (<b>Figure</b>&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0001"><b>1</b>A,B</a>). Upon exposure to H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, its absorption peak underwent a bathochromic shift to 710&nbsp;nm, and the fluorescence was rapidly enhanced by 10-fold at 730&nbsp;nm, illustrating its high sensitivity to H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>. The response mechanism of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> to H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> stimulation was subsequently confirmed. Synergetic analysis using HPLC and HRMS-ESI data provided evidence for successful release of hCy (retention time at 6.5&nbsp;min, m/z = 496.2050 [M]<sup>+</sup>) from hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> (retention time at 6.8&nbsp;min, m/z = 630.2607 [M]<sup>+</sup>) after incubating with H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0001">1E</a>; Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S22</a>, Supporting Information). The detailed investigation revealed a strong positive correlation between higher levels of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> and enhanced NIR fluorescence (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0001">1C</a>). This correlation was particularly pronounced at low concentrations of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, exhibiting a positive linearity (y = 844.60073x+3035.4310) with a limit of detection as low as 42&nbsp;n<span class="smallCaps">m</span> (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S1</a>, Supporting Information). Kinetic studies demonstrated that the NIR fluorescence of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> rapidly increased in the presence of high concentration of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, reaching a plateau within 10&nbsp;min (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0001">1D</a>), and the photostability studies revealed that the fluorescence intensity of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> remained constant in both the absence and presence of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> after 3&nbsp;h of excitation (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S2A</a>, Supporting Information). In addition, phenylboronic acid can undergo selective oxidation by H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, ensuring the exceptional selectivity of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>. Its fluorescence is exclusively activated by H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> and remains unresponsive to other potential interferents commonly found in complex biological environments, rendering it suitable for precise imaging of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> in intricate biological systems (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0001">1F</a>). The fluorescence response of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> to H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> was minimally affected by variations in pH from 3.0 to 12.0 and serum concentrations, making it highly suitable for precise imaging of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> in complex biological systems (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S2B,C</a>, Supporting Information).</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="smll202401282-fig-0001"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/03cb77e8-c5c4-4c7e-9520-37b06d62899b/smll202401282-fig-0001-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/03cb77e8-c5c4-4c7e-9520-37b06d62899b/smll202401282-fig-0001-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/af479cda-a576-4a98-ab3c-7bc3abb16a3b/smll202401282-fig-0001-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/03cb77e8-c5c4-4c7e-9520-37b06d62899b/smll202401282-fig-0001-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">Figure 1<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=smll202401282-fig-0001&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fsmll.202401282" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">A) Absorption and B) fluorescence spectra of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> (10 µм) in the absence and presence of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> (50 µм). λ<sub>ex</sub> = 680&nbsp;nm. C) Fluorescence spectra after adding different concentrations of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> (0-50 µм). D) Kinetic studies of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> to different concentrations of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>. E) Chromatograms of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> reacting with H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> and hCy. λ<sub>abs</sub> = 600&nbsp;nm. F) Fluorescence response of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> to different analytes (50 µм) and enzymes (1 U·mL<sup>−1</sup>). λ<sub>ex</sub> = 680&nbsp;nm, λ<sub>em</sub> = 730&nbsp;nm.</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
         </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="smll202401282-sec-0040">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="smll202401282-sec-0040-title">2.2 Preparation of the Liver-Targeted Sequence-Activated Fluorogenic Nanoprobe hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP</h3>
            
            <p>The preparation procedure of the nanoprobe hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP is depicted in <b>Figure</b>&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0002">2</a>. Lipid nanoparticle components (ionizable lipids, cholesterol, DSPC and DMG-PEG2000) with optimized proportions were pre-mixed with the small molecule agent hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>. Subsequently, a typical solvent injection procedure was employed to formulate the liver-targeting fluorogenic nanoprobe, hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP (Table <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S1</a>, Supporting Information). The iLNPs loaded with non-caged dyes hCy (hCy@iLNP) were prepared to concurrently assess the physicochemical properties of the iLNP. Firstly, the physicochemical properties of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP including hydrodynamic size and zeta potential were investigated. iLNPs exhibited a DLS size of 58.87&nbsp;nm and a zeta potential of 8&nbsp;mV at neutral pH (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0002">2B,E</a>). Upon formation of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP, its diameter increased to 76.76&nbsp;nm with a zeta potential of 7&nbsp;mV (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0002">2C,E</a>). While the formation of hCy@iLNP with a diameter of 61.14&nbsp;nm was observed (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S3</a>, Supporting Information). Small molecule hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> displayed a zeta potential of 13&nbsp;mV at neutral pH due to its positive charge from hCy dyes (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0002">2E</a>). These findings confirm the successful formation of the fluorogenic nanoprobe hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP. Furthermore, measurements on loading efficiency revealed high values for small molecule hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> at approximately 85% (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S4</a>, Supporting Information). The morphological characteristics of proposed hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP were investigated using transmission electron microscopy (TEM), which determined the particle size as 74.5 ± 6.1&nbsp;nm with uniform distribution and discernible bilayer structures (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0002">2D</a>; Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S5</a>, Supporting Information). These results indicate successful encapsulation of small molecule agent hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> by iLNPs, resulting in targeted nanoprobe.</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="smll202401282-fig-0002"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/7050fb85-ba3a-4f17-b418-1357ae88470c/smll202401282-fig-0002-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/7050fb85-ba3a-4f17-b418-1357ae88470c/smll202401282-fig-0002-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/b5cc1e16-a0f8-41e7-ace9-1f8e1c3bab07/smll202401282-fig-0002-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/7050fb85-ba3a-4f17-b418-1357ae88470c/smll202401282-fig-0002-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">Figure 2<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=smll202401282-fig-0002&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fsmll.202401282" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">A) Composition and preparation of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP. The hydrodynamic diameter distribution of B) blank lipids and C) hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP. D) TEM image of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP. E) Zeta potential of blank lipids, hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> and of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP. F) DLS sizes of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP (10 µм), hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP (10 µм) treated with H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> (50 µм) and hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP (10 µм) treated with surfactant Triton X-100. G) Absorption and H) fluorescence spectra of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP (10 µм) in the absence and presence of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> (50 µм) and Triton X-100 (0.5%, v/v).</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
            <p>Then the spectral responses of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP were observed under different stimulations. hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP exhibited an absorbance band centered ≈600&nbsp;nm with negligible NIR fluorescence, which can be attributed to the caged states of internal small molecule fluorogenic agents (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0002">2G,H</a>). However, there was no significant changes observed in the absorption and fluorescence spectra after H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> treatment, possibly due to the insensitive of the double-layer structure toward H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>. The isolation of membrane structure in response to H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> was the initial step taken to validate the hypothesis of sequence-activated fluorescent nanoprobes. In addition, a surfactant Triton X-100-treated group was included. Incubation with Triton X-100 destroyed the lipid bilayer structure of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP and facilitated the release of the payload fluorogenic agent hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0002">2F</a>). Simultaneously, there was a significant increase in fluorescence at 745&nbsp;nm upon H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> stimulation, indicating that high-contrast NIR fluorescence signal generated from hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP can be activated by H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> after fluorogenic agents are successfully released (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0002">2H</a>). However, no significant changes observed in the fluorescence spectra of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP upon sequential treatment with H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, NAC, and Triton X-100, which demonstrates the excellent preservation of the bilayer phospholipid structure and the sequence-dependent activation behavior of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S6</a>, Supporting Information). The hCy@iLNP further validated the hypothesis that iLNPs induce fluorescence quenching, as evidenced by the release of hCy with a distinct fluorescence signal at 745&nbsp;nm following disruption of the lipid bilayer structure (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S7</a>, Supporting Information). The fluorescent response to H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> remained unaffected by pH variations ranging from 3.0 to 12.0, thereby highlighting the exceptional capability of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP for detecting H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> under physiological conditions (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S2D</a>, Supporting Information). In addition, the selectivity of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP was demonstrated by its exclusive fluorescence activation upon treatment with H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> and Triton X-100, while exhibiting negligible fluorescence response toward other ions, ROS, reactive nitrogen species (RNS), and reactive sulfur species (RSS) (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S8</a>, Supporting Information). Furthermore, we tested the stability of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP and hCy@iLNP, found that its diameter and fluorescence intensity remained unchanged even after incubation in PBS for up to 14 days (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S9</a>, Supporting Information). These results demonstrate hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP are sequence-activated fluorescent nanoprobes with the immense potential for sensitive and accurate detection of ROS levels and oxidative stress in intricate biosystems.</p>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="smll202401282-sec-0050">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="smll202401282-sec-0050-title">2.3 Subcellular Localization Analysis of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP and Exploration of Delivery Mechanism</h3>
            
            <p>The biocompatibility of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP was first assessed using the standard CCK-8 assay. Following a 24&nbsp;h incubation with either hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP or payload agents hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, cell viabilities remained above 90% across various molecular concentrations, indicating negligible cytotoxicity (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S10</a>, Supporting Information). Additionally, there were no noticeable differences in the cytotoxicity of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP compared to payload agents hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, due to the similarity between cytomembrane and the lipid bilayer structures of iLNPs. These findings confirm the excellent biocompatibility of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP, making it suitable for subsequent cellular imaging.</p>
            
            <p>Efficient cellular entry and internalization are crucial factors for effective delivery of fluorogenic agents as well as ROS imaging. To further investigate internalization behavior of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP, HepG2 cells were incubated with hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP and Lyso-Tracker Green for cell fluorescence imaging. NIR fluorescence of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP within cells increased over time, suggesting that hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP were continuously internalized by HepG2 cells (<b>Figure</b>&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0003"><b>3</b>B</a>; Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S11</a>, Supporting Information). A strong overlap was found by analyzing the signals from hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP and lysosome, and the highest level of colocalization between hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP and lysosome occurred around 4&nbsp;h (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0003">3D</a>). Meanwhile, we investigated the colocalization level of payload agents hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> with lysosomes (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S12</a>, Supporting Information). The fluorescence emitted by payload agents hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> accumulated within lysosomes and overlapped well with the green fluorescence from Lyso-Tracker. After accumulation in the endosomes or lysosomes, the ionizable tertiary amines of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP causes protonation when the ambient pH below their p<i>K</i>a, facilitating the formation of lipid pairs with negatively charged lipids in the lysosomal membrane as well as increasing inward flow of small molecule probe (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0003">3A</a>).<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0015" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0015_R_d80835714e1246" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 15 - Bioact. Mater. Bioact. Mater. Exploration Sci. Adv.">15</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> exhibits efficient cytoplasmic retention and maintains photostability for over 8 h both before and after liposome encapsulation, facilitating the visualization of intracellular oxidative stress through fluorescence imaging.</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="smll202401282-fig-0003"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/f86148b0-5aa0-4849-a016-73e8232912bf/smll202401282-fig-0003-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/f86148b0-5aa0-4849-a016-73e8232912bf/smll202401282-fig-0003-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/65b577cf-bda1-46f6-98d1-e700ba138a4e/smll202401282-fig-0003-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/f86148b0-5aa0-4849-a016-73e8232912bf/smll202401282-fig-0003-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">Figure 3<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=smll202401282-fig-0003&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fsmll.202401282" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">Exploration of the delivery behavior of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP. A) Schematic illustration of iLNPs-mediated lysosome targeting of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> and its application in oxidative stress imaging. B) Localization of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP (10 µм) in HepG2 cells at different time points. C) Validation of sequence activation of hCy@iLNP (10 µм) and hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP (10 µм) in HepG2 cells at different time points, the cells were pretreated with NAC (1 mм) for 2&nbsp;h. D) Colocalization correlations between hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP and lysosome. E) Fluorescence intensities of HepG2 cells treated with hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP and hCy@iLNP at different time points. λ<sub>ex</sub> = 639&nbsp;nm, λ<sub>em</sub> = 663–738&nbsp;nm. Scale bar: 30&nbsp;µm.</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
            <p>To validate the hypothesis of sequence-activated iLNP at the cellular level, the fluorescence phenomena of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP and hCy@iLNP in HepG2 cells at different times were investigated. Prior to incubation with iLNPs, HepG2 cells were pretreated with N-Acety-L-Cysteine (NAC) for 2&nbsp;h to eliminate ROS interference and exclude any secondary response of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> due to expressed H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0017" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0017_R_d80835714e1321" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 17 - Cell Death Dis.">17</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> No red fluorescence signal was observed when hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP and hCy@iLNP were just incubated with the cells (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0003">3C</a>). However, as the cultivation period progressed, gradual enhancement in red fluorescence was observed in hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP and hCy@iLNP treated group, pointing that iLNPs were progressively internalized by cells to release hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> and hCy. This phenomenon was further illustrated by a statistical diagram, which proved the effectively quenching of fluorogenic agents by stable bilayer of iLNPs (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0003">3E</a>). Moreover, cells treated with hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP exhibited extremely weak fluorescence, indicating efficient scavenging of ROS lead to incomplete reaction between hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP and H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>. These results verified that within the cells, there are two distinct processes involved in the action of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0003">3A</a>): first, lipid nanoparticle dissociate the membrane of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP to release hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, subsequently, H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> cleaves off cage positions within cells leading to ultimate release of red fluorescence. The sequence-activated nanoprobe hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP possesses great potential for eliminating interference and enabling more accurate biological imaging.</p>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="smll202401282-sec-0060">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="smll202401282-sec-0060-title">2.4 Fluorescence Imaging of Cellular H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub></h3>
            
            <p>The initiation and progression of ILD are heavily influenced by intracellular oxidative stress. Therefore, the H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-responsive fluorogenic nanoprobe hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP was utilized to assess ROS levels in various inflammatory cell models (<b>Figure</b>&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0004"><b>4</b>A</a>). An inflammatory cell modal was induced using lipopolysaccharide (LPS).<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0018" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0018_R_d80835714e1410" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 18 - Cell Rep. Adv. Funct. Mater.">18</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> As depicted in Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0004">4B</a>, upon incubation with hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP, weak cytoplasmic fluorescence was observed in HepG2 cells due to relatively low H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> levels under normal conditions. In LPS-treated inflammatory cells, brighter fluorescence indicated an increase in H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> concentration, suggesting that H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> can serve as an indicator for screening inflammatory cells. Additionally, the intensity of fluorescence signals is proportional to the dosage of LPS, indicating the capability of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP to detect small fluctuations in H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> levels in living cells (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0004">4D</a>). Antioxidant studies were conducted to validate the selectivity of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP for detecting oxidative stress. As illustrated in Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0004">4B,D</a>, pretreatment of HepG2 cells with NAC, an antioxidant approved by the U. S. Food and Drug Administration, effectively attenuated the fluorescence resulting from LPS-induced oxidative damage. In short, these observations substantiate the exceptional cell delivery capabilities of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP and its selective detection ability for H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> and oxidative damage in living cells. Furthermore, overdose of acetaminophen (APAP) can result in elevated intracellular ROS levels, triggering oxidative stress and causing cellular inflammatory damage.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0019" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0019_R_d80835714e1464" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 19 - Toxicology Redox Biol.">19</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Compared to control cells displaying weak fluorescence, APAP treatment significantly increased intracellular fluorescence, and higher concentrations of APAP lead to a higher fluorescence, indicating the occurrence of oxidative stress and inflammatory damage conditions (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0004">4C,E</a>). Conversely, intracellular fluorescence was suppressed in the presence of NAC. Furthermore, we also investigated the fluorescence changes of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> in living cells following carious treatments, which exhibited congruence with hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP-treated samples (Figures <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S13</a> and <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S14</a>, Supporting Information). The commercial probe DCFH-DA was employed to investigate the impact of both hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> and hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP on intracellular ROS levels within hepatocytes. No significant enhancement in green fluorescence of DCFH-DA was observed in HL-7702 cells treated with either probe (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S15</a>, Supporting Information). To conclude, hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP enables visualization of intracellular oxidative stress through fluorescence imaging and serves as an efficient tool for accurate detection of cellular inflammation.</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="smll202401282-fig-0004"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/e8c82f3b-3436-4d63-9259-3a031f638ef5/smll202401282-fig-0004-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/e8c82f3b-3436-4d63-9259-3a031f638ef5/smll202401282-fig-0004-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/20692734-558c-43bd-a880-c3de91382bc0/smll202401282-fig-0004-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/e8c82f3b-3436-4d63-9259-3a031f638ef5/smll202401282-fig-0004-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">Figure 4<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=smll202401282-fig-0004&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fsmll.202401282" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">In vitro fluorescence imaging and quantification analysis of HepG2 cells with different treatments before incubation with hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP (10 µм) for 2&nbsp;h. A) The imaging mechanism of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP in living cells. B) Fluorescence images of LPS-treated inflammatory damage cells: a) Intact cells, b–d) cells pretreated with LPS (0.3, 1 and 3&nbsp;µg&nbsp;mL<sup>−1</sup>) for 2&nbsp;h, e,f) cells pretreated with NAC (1 mм) for 1&nbsp;h before treatment with LPS (1 and 3&nbsp;µg&nbsp;mL<sup>−1</sup>) for 2&nbsp;h; C) Fluorescence images of APAP-treated inflammatory damage cells: i) Intact cells; ii,iii) cells pretreated with APAP (0.5 and 1 mм) for 1&nbsp;h; iv) cells pretreated with NAC (1 mм) for 1&nbsp;h before treatment with APAP (1 mм) for 1&nbsp;h. D,E) Quantitative analysis of the corresponding fluorescence intensity in panels of B and C, <sup>*</sup>P &lt; 0.05 and **P &lt; 0.01. λ<sub>ex</sub> = 639&nbsp;nm, λ<sub>em</sub> = 663–738&nbsp;nm. Scale bar: 30&nbsp;µm.</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
         </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="smll202401282-sec-0070">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="smll202401282-sec-0070-title">2.5 In Vivo Imaging of APAP-Induced Inflammatory Liver Injury</h3>
            
            <p>The early stages of ILD are characterized by varying degrees of oxidative stress in hepatocytes, which serve as a significant indicator for the accurate and early detection of ILD.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0020" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0020_R_d80835714e1552" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 20 - Redox Biol. Anal. Chem.">20</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Building upon the exceptional responsiveness of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP to H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> in vitro, our focus then shifts toward evaluating the in vivo imaging performance of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP in animal models of ILD. Previous reports have demonstrated that iLNPs can form a “protein corona” by absorbing proteins during blood circulation, which can influence their biological distribution behavior.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0012" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0012_R_d80835714e1570" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 12 - Acc. Chem. Res. Angew. Chem., Int. Ed. Nat. Nanotechnol.">12</a>, <a href="#smll202401282-bib-0021" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0021_R_d80835714e1570" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 21 - Sci. Adv. Sci. Adv.">21</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Abundant binding of apolipoprotein E onto iLNPs plays a dominant role in receptor-meditated liver targeting, facilitating efficient delivery and accurate imaging of fluorescent payloads within the liver.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#smll202401282-bib-0015" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll202401282-bib-0015_R_d80835714e1576" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 15 - Bioact. Mater. Bioact. Mater. Exploration Sci. Adv.">15</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Then, the potential of iLNPs in liver-targeted delivery were investigated in vivo. Mice were intraperitoneally injected with an overdose of APAP to establish a model of inflammatory liver injury (<b>Figure</b>&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0005"><b>5</b>A</a>). The liver tissue from the model mice exhibited evident morphological abnormalities, including massive hemorrhage, indicating the presence of inflammation and hepatocyte injury (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0005">5E</a>).</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="smll202401282-fig-0005"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/1c14ee38-ebd3-4ca3-b18d-8561fdc07c8c/smll202401282-fig-0005-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/1c14ee38-ebd3-4ca3-b18d-8561fdc07c8c/smll202401282-fig-0005-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/6dc58df8-2bbe-4e59-9519-e1abd373577a/smll202401282-fig-0005-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/1c14ee38-ebd3-4ca3-b18d-8561fdc07c8c/smll202401282-fig-0005-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">Figure 5<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=smll202401282-fig-0005&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fsmll.202401282" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">In vivo fluorescence imaging of APAP-induced inflammatory liver injury. A) Schematic illustration of mouse model construction and in vivo fluorescence imaging of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>. B) In vivo fluorescence imaging. Mice were intraperitoneally injected with APAP (300&nbsp;mg&nbsp;kg<sup>−1</sup>) for 2&nbsp;h before intravenous injection with hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP (containing hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> 100 µм, 100&nbsp;µL) or hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> (100 µм, 100&nbsp;µL). C) Quantitative analysis of the corresponding fluorescence intensity in the liver regions in panels of (B), <sup>**</sup>P &lt; 0.01 and n.s.: not significant. D) Ex vivo fluorescence imaging of major organs dissected from mice after treatment with two probes for 2&nbsp;h. E) Histological study of liver tissues from mice treated with PBS (200&nbsp;mL) and APAP (300&nbsp;mg&nbsp;kg<sup>−1</sup>) for 6&nbsp;h; black arrow: hemorrhage. Scale bar: 50&nbsp;µm. λ<sub>ex</sub> = 640&nbsp;nm, λ<sub>em</sub> = 740&nbsp;nm.</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
            <p>In APAP-treated mice, the administration of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP led to a sustained high-intensity fluorescence even after 1.5&nbsp;h post-injection (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0005">5B</a>). However, in healthy mice, the fluorescence was comparatively weaker and exhibited rapid fluorescence decay due to lower levels of ROS within normal liver. The quantification of fluorescence revealed that the NIR fluorescence in the livers of APAP-treated mice was twice as high as that in healthy mice at 1.5&nbsp;h (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0005">5C</a>). Furthermore, after intravenous injection of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP, significant increases in hepatic fluorescence were observed both in vivo and ex vivo imaging (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0005">5D</a>). The substantial increase in fluorescence revealed that the levels of hepatic H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> and oxidative stress increased after APAP treatment. However, there was no significant difference between the two groups of mice after 2&nbsp;h treatment with the small molecule agent hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0005">5B,C</a>). The preliminary study has demonstrated that iLNPs nanoprobes not only effectively enhance hepatic accumulation of fluorogenic agents, but also improve the highly sensitive and precise detection of ROS levels in the liver. The biosecurity of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP was confirmed through histological analysis, hemolysis analysis, body weight measurement, blood routine examination, and blood biochemical analysis (Figures <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S16–S18</a> and Tables <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S2</a> and <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S3</a>, Supporting Information). Notably, hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP achieved faster and more precise detection of inflammatory liver injury at only 2&nbsp;h post-APAP treatment, possibly due to the relatively early appearance of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> in inflammatory liver injury. Overall, iLNPs serve as a versatile and superior nanocarrier for facile construction of liver-targeting imaging nanoprobes, enabling early indication of oxidative stress in the early stages of ILD.</p>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="smll202401282-sec-0080">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="smll202401282-sec-0080-title">2.6 In Vivo Imaging of LPS-Induced Liver Inflammation</h3>
            
            <p>The universality and potential of liver-targeted hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP for detecting different degrees of liver inflammation were investigated in another mouse model, that was established by intraperitoneal injection of varying dosages of LPS in mice (<b>Figure</b>&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0006"><b>6</b>A</a>). Subsequently, hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP was intravenously administrated for targeted fluorescence imaging. As depicted in Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0006">6A</a>, the fluorescence signal in the liver region was significantly increased in LPS-treated mice compared to healthy mice after the injection of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP. Comprehensive statistical analysis revealed a positive correlation between the fluorescence signal of the liver and the dose of LPS injected (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0006">6C</a>), indicating that iLNPs have the ability to differentiate various degrees of liver inflammation. Ex vivo imaging confirmed that the fluorescence signals primarily originated from the livers of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP-treated mice, and these changes in liver fluorescence were consistent with results obtained from in vivo imaging (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0006">6D</a>). The analysis of liver H&amp;E staining results revealed that even after high doses (20&nbsp;mg&nbsp;kg<sup>−1</sup>) of LPS treatment for 2&nbsp;h, there was no significant evidence of liver inflammation observed (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S19</a>, Supporting Information). This finding indicated that hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP can sensitively identify varying levels of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> and diagnose liver inflammation prior to any overt pathological changes occured. In conclusion, the combination of activatable fluorogenic agents and efficient liver-targeted delivery system can effectively enhance the sensitive detection of hepatic biomarkers in vivo, enabling early and accurate identification of different degrees of liver inflammation according to fluorescence signals in the liver regions.</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="smll202401282-fig-0006"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/7185bf87-30a7-4142-a2c2-e9ec13ec5fce/smll202401282-fig-0006-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/7185bf87-30a7-4142-a2c2-e9ec13ec5fce/smll202401282-fig-0006-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/cac12ef2-d7c0-468f-b247-71a9418d69b1/smll202401282-fig-0006-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/7185bf87-30a7-4142-a2c2-e9ec13ec5fce/smll202401282-fig-0006-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">Figure 6<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=smll202401282-fig-0006&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fsmll.202401282" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">In vivo fluorescence imaging of LPS-induced liver inflammation. A) Mice were intraperitoneally injected with different amounts of LPS (0, 5, 10, or 20&nbsp;mg&nbsp;kg<sup>−1</sup>) for 2&nbsp;h before intravenous injection with hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP (containing hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> 100 µм, 100&nbsp;µL). B) Mice were intraperitoneally injected with LPS (10&nbsp;mg&nbsp;kg<sup>−1</sup>) for 1, 2 and 4&nbsp;h before intravenous injection with hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP (100 µм, 100&nbsp;µL). C,E) Quantitative analysis of the corresponding fluorescence intensity in the liver regions in panels of A and B, *P &lt; 0.05 and **P &lt; 0.01. D,F) Ex vivo fluorescence imaging of livers dissected from mice after different treatments. G) Histological study of the liver sections harvested from mice treated with LPS (10&nbsp;mg&nbsp;k<sup>−1</sup>g) for different times (1, 2, 4, 12, 24&nbsp;h); blue arrow: focal necrosis, red arrow: inflammatory infiltration, black arrow: hemorrhage. Scale bar: 50&nbsp;µm. λ<sub>ex</sub> = 640&nbsp;nm, λ<sub>em</sub> = 740&nbsp;nm.</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
            <p>We conducted further investigation into the levels of oxidative stress in the progression of liver inflammation using our nanoprobe. Mice were intraperitoneally injected with 10&nbsp;mg&nbsp;Kg<sup>−1</sup> LPS for different times to induce liver inflammation, followed by intravenous injection of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP to detect the degree. As shown in Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0006">6B</a>, NIR fluorescence images demonstrated the temporally resolved activation of fluorescence triggered by ROS in vivo, the fluorescence signal in the liver region showed enhanced intensity with prolonged administration of LPS compared with healthy mice. The fluorescence signal in the hepatic region exhibited a gradual increase over time. Similarly, statistical analysis also demonstrated a progressive elevation in the fluorescence signal of LPS-induced hepatic inflammation mice (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0006">6E</a>). These results suggest that the liver-targeting nanoprobe hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP is capable of sensitively detecting small fluctuations in H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> levels in vivo. Ex vivo imaging revealed a time-resolved fluorescence intensity in dissected livers from LPS-treated mice, which was higher compared to control mice (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0006">6F</a>). To validate the establishment of the LPS-induced liver inflammation model, mouse livers were dissected after 1, 2, 4, 12, and 24&nbsp;h of LPS treatment for H&amp;E staining. The results revealed distinct inflammatory symptoms such as cell necrosis and blood stasis after 12 and 24&nbsp;h of LPS treatment compared to the absence of visible symptoms following short-time exposure (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll202401282-fig-0006">6G</a>). Additionally, hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP effectively detected abnormal ROS expression in liver tissue at an early stage of liver inflammation, preceding both blood tests and the onset of inflammation (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S20</a>, Supporting Information). In conclusion, hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP, with its primary liver-targeting features, can serve as a potent tool for early and accurate diagnosis of LPS-induced hepatic inflammation, demonstrating excellent feasibility for precise imaging of various ILD.</p>
            </section>
         </section>
      <section class="article-section__content" id="smll202401282-sec-0090">
         
         <h2 class="article-section__title section__title section1" id="smll202401282-sec-0090-title">3 Conclusion</h2>
         
         <p>In summary, we propose an effective utilization of iLNPs as liver-targeted carriers for constructing sequence-activated fluorogenic nanoprobes. Based on the nanoplatform, liver-targeted ROS-activated fluorogenic nanoprobe hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP was constructed for in vivo imaging of oxidative stress within the inflammatory liver. Upon administration of hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP, the stable encapsulation and apolipoprotein E recruitment by cytomembrane-mimicking iLNPs carriers overcome biodistribution barriers and achieve efficient accumulation of fluorogenic reporters in the liver. Nanoprobe hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>@iLNP effectively releases hCy-H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> through primary membrane dissociation, then exhibits a sensitive fluorescent response to H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> after release and enables sensitive detection of small fluctuations of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> in inflammatory cells. In vivo biodistribution investigation demonstrates the satisfactory delivery capabilities of liver-targeted iLNPs, endowing high liver accumulation and robust imaging capability for intrahepatic oxidative stress. Such properties empower the early and accurate identification of oxidative stress and inflammation progresses in the mice treated with different concentrations of LPS, and has a high contrast and reliable visualization of APAP-induced inflammatory liver injury. We anticipate this organ-targeted fluorescent nanoplatform can furnish insights into biomarkers and provide a versatile strategy available for the precise diagnosis of other organ diseases.</p>
         </section>
      <div class="article-section__content">

        </p>
